</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Legacy">Legacy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ladislaus_I_of_Hungary&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Legacy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Gentiana_cruciata_030705b.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/60/Gentiana_cruciata_030705b.jpg/220px-Gentiana_cruciata_030705b.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="255" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/60/Gentiana_cruciata_030705b.jpg/330px-Gentiana_cruciata_030705b.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/60/Gentiana_cruciata_030705b.jpg/440px-Gentiana_cruciata_030705b.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1383" data-file-height="1600" /></a><figcaption>A blue-flowered <i><a href="/wiki/Gentiana_cruciata" title="Gentiana cruciata">Gentiana cruciata</a></i> (Star Gentian), traditionally known in Hungary as "St. Ladislaus's Herb" (<a href="/wiki/Hungarian_language" title="Hungarian language">Hungarian</a>: <i lang="hu">Szent László füve)</i></figcaption></figure>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Consolidation_of_the_Christian_monarchy">Consolidation of the Christian monarchy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ladislaus_I_of_Hungary&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Consolidation of the Christian monarchy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>For centuries, hagiographers and historians have emphasized Ladislaus's prominent role in the consolidation of the Christian monarchy.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKlaniczay2002173_108-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKlaniczay2002173-108">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup> The chronicles also stressed his <i>idoneitas</i>, or personal suitability, to reign, because the legitimacy of his rule was questionable.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKlaniczay2002173_108-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKlaniczay2002173-108">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup> The <i>Illuminated Chronicle</i> clearly states that Ladislaus knew that "the right of law between him and [Solomon] was not on his side but only the force of fact".<sup id="cite_ref-Chronicon_94_133_128_54-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Chronicon_94_133_128-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKlaniczay2002173_108-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKlaniczay2002173-108">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>After Ladislaus's victories over the Pechenegs and the Cumans, the nomadic peoples of the Pontic steppes stopped invading Hungary until the Mongol invasion of 1241.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEngel200137_109-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEngel200137-109">&#91;109&#93;</a></sup> Kristó suggests that the <a href="/wiki/Sz%C3%A9kelys" title="Székelys">Székely people</a>—a community of Hungarian-speaking warriors—started settling the easternmost borderlands under Ladislaus.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKristóMakk1996121_59-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKristóMakk1996121-59">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEngel2001115_110-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEngel2001115-110">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup> The "historic association of the Kingdom of Hungary and <a href="/wiki/Croatia" title="Croatia">Croatia</a>", which ended in 1918, began with Ladislaus's conquest of Croatia.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKontler199963_55-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKontler199963-55">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> His conquest marked the beginning of a period of Hungarian expansion, which ensured that Hungary developed into a leading <a href="/wiki/Central_Europe" title="Central Europe">Central European</a> power during the following centuries.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEngel200137_109-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEngel200137-109">&#91;109&#93;</a></sup> It became a customary rite for a newly crowned Hungarian king to make a pilgrimage to Ladislaus's shrine at <a href="/wiki/V%C3%A1rad" title="Várad">Várad</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEngel200132_37-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEngel200132-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Louis_I_of_Hungary" title="Louis I of Hungary">Louis I of Hungary</a>, who made many attempts to expand his territory in the Balkan Peninsula, showed a special respect for Ladislaus.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECartledge201136_111-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECartledge201136-111">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>Hungary had never had as great as king, so they repute<br />And the land thereafter never bore that much and splendid fruit.</p><div class="templatequotecite">—&#8202;<cite><a href="/wiki/Gallus_Anonymus" title="Gallus Anonymus">Gallus Anonymus</a>: <i><a href="/wiki/Gesta_principum_Polonorum" title="Gesta principum Polonorum">The Deeds of the Princes of the Poles</a></i><sup id="cite_ref-Gallus_27_97_7-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Gallus_27_97-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup></cite></div></blockquote>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Veneration">Veneration</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ladislaus_I_of_Hungary&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Veneration">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1033289096">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Saint_Ladislaus_legend" title="Saint Ladislaus legend">Saint Ladislaus legend</a></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1066479718"><table class="infobox vcard"><tbody><tr><th colspan="2" class="infobox-above n" style="background-color:gold;"><div style="display:inline;" class="fn">St. Ladislaus</div></th></tr><tr><td colspan="2" class="infobox-image"><span class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Frameless"><a href="/wiki/File:LaszloOradea.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2e/LaszloOradea.jpg/220px-LaszloOradea.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="293" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2e/LaszloOradea.jpg/330px-LaszloOradea.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2e/LaszloOradea.jpg/440px-LaszloOradea.jpg 2x" data-file-width="480" data-file-height="640" /></a></span><div class="infobox-caption">Saint Ladislaus's reliquary of the late 19th-century (in the Cathedral-Basilica of Oradea, Romania)</div></td></tr><tr><th colspan="2" class="infobox-header" style="background-color:gold;">King and Confessor</th></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Venerated&#160;in</th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Church" class="mw-redirect" title="Roman Catholic Church">Roman Catholic Church</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Canonization" title="Canonization">Canonized</a></th><td class="infobox-data">27 June 1192 (dubious) by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Celestine_III" title="Pope Celestine III">Pope Celestine III</a> (dubious)</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><span class="nowrap">Major <a href="/wiki/Shrine" title="Shrine">shrine</a></span></th><td class="infobox-data">Cathedral-Basilica of <a href="/wiki/Oradea" title="Oradea">Oradea</a>, <a href="/wiki/Romania" title="Romania">Romania</a> and Cathedral-Basilica of <a href="/wiki/Gy%C5%91r" title="Győr">Győr</a>, <a href="/wiki/Hungary" title="Hungary">Hungary</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints" title="Calendar of saints">Feast</a></th><td class="infobox-data">27 June</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Saint_symbolism" title="Saint symbolism">Attributes</a></th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Crown_of_St._Stephen" class="mw-redirect" title="Crown of St. Stephen">Holy Crown of St. Stephen I</a><br /><a href="/wiki/Long_sword" class="mw-redirect" title="Long sword">Long sword</a><br /> <a href="/wiki/Angel" title="Angel">Two angels</a><br /><a href="/wiki/Banner" title="Banner">Banner</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Patron_saint" title="Patron saint">Patronage</a></th><td class="infobox-data">Patron Saint of <a href="/wiki/Hungary" title="Hungary">Hungary</a><br />Patron Saint of the <a href="/wiki/Sz%C3%A9kelys" title="Székelys">Székelys</a><br />Protector against pestilence</td></tr></tbody></table>
<p>Gábor Klaniczay emphasizes that Ladislaus "seemed expressly designed to personify the knight-king ideal" of his age.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKlaniczay2002173_108-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKlaniczay2002173-108">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup> During the reign of Ladislaus's successor, <a href="/wiki/Coloman_the_Learned" class="mw-redirect" title="Coloman the Learned">Coloman the Learned</a>, Bishop <a href="/wiki/Hartvik" title="Hartvik">Hartvik</a> said that Ladislaus's "character was distinguished by the respectability of morals and remarkable for the splendor of his virtues".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKristóMakk1996122_81-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKristóMakk1996122-81">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-112" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-112">&#91;112&#93;</a></sup> The so-called <i>Gesta Ladislai regis</i> ("The Deeds of King Ladislaus"), which are the texts about Ladislaus's life and reign preserved in 14th-century Hungarian chronicles, were written during Coloman's rule.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKlaniczay2002173_108-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKlaniczay2002173-108">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup> Five significant events of Ladislaus's life, which were not included in his official legend, were only preserved in the <i>Gesta</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKlaniczay2002176–177_22-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKlaniczay2002176–177-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The most popular story describes Ladislaus's fight with a "<a href="/wiki/Turkic_peoples" title="Turkic peoples">Cuman</a>" warrior after the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Kerl%C3%A9s" title="Battle of Kerlés">Battle of Kerlés</a> (at present-day <a href="/wiki/Chirale%C8%99" class="mw-redirect" title="Chiraleș">Chiraleș</a>, Romania) in 1068.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKlaniczay2002177,_190_113-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKlaniczay2002177,_190-113">&#91;113&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELászló1996142_114-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELászló1996142-114">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup> In the battle, the united armies of Solomon, Géza and Ladislaus routed a band of <a href="/wiki/Pechenegs" title="Pechenegs">Pechenegs</a> or <a href="/wiki/Oghuz_Turks" title="Oghuz Turks">Oghuz Turks</a> who were plundering the eastern parts of the kingdom.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKristóMakk1996107_4-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKristóMakk1996107-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBárány2012339–340_115-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBárány2012339–340-115">&#91;115&#93;</a></sup> According to the version recorded in the <i>Illuminated Chronicle</i>, Ladislaus spotted a pagan warrior fleeing from the battlefield with a captive Hungarian maiden.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELászló1996142_114-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELászló1996142-114">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup> Ladislaus pursued the "Cuman", but he could not stop him.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELászló1996143_116-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELászló1996143-116">&#91;116&#93;</a></sup> On Ladislaus's advice, the maiden pulled the warrior off his horse, allowing Ladislaus to kill the "Cuman" after a long fight on the ground.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELászló1996143_116-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELászló1996143-116">&#91;116&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKlaniczay2002190–191_117-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKlaniczay2002190–191-117">&#91;117&#93;</a></sup> Archaeologist <a href="/wiki/Gyula_L%C3%A1szl%C3%B3" title="Gyula László">Gyula László</a> says that murals depicting this legend in medieval churches preserved the elements of pagan myths, including a "struggle between forces of light and darkness".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELászló1996142_114-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELászló1996142-114">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKlaniczay2002192–193_118-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKlaniczay2002192–193-118">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>[The] most blessed Duke Ladislaus saw one of the pagans who was carrying off on his horse a beautiful Hungarian girl. The saintly Duke Ladislaus thought that it was the daughter of the <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Diocese_of_Oradea_Mare" title="Roman Catholic Diocese of Oradea Mare">Bishop of Warad</a>, and although he was seriously wounded, he swiftly pursued him on his horse, which he called by the name of Zug. When he caught up with him and wished to spear him, he could not do so, for neither could his own horse go any faster nor did the other's horse yield any ground, but there remained the distance of a man's arm between his spear and the Coman's back. So the saintly Duke Ladislaus shouted to the girl and said: "Fair sister, take hold of the Coman by his belt and throw yourself to the ground." Which she did; and the saintly Duke Ladislaus was about to spear him as he lay upon the ground, for he wished to kill him. But the girl strongly pleaded with him not to kill him, but to let him go. Whence it is to be seen that there is no faith in women; for it was probably because of strong carnal love that she wished him to go free. But after having fought for a long time with him and unmanned him, the saintly Duke killed him. But the girl was not the bishop's daughter.</p><div class="templatequotecite">—&#8202;<cite><i><a href="/wiki/Illuminated_Chronicle" class="mw-redirect" title="Illuminated Chronicle">The Hungarian Illuminated Chronicle</a></i><sup id="cite_ref-119" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-119">&#91;119&#93;</a></sup></cite></div></blockquote>
<p>During the reign of <a href="/wiki/Stephen_II_of_Hungary" title="Stephen II of Hungary">Stephen II of Hungary</a>, Ladislaus's shrine in the cathedral of Várad became a preferred venue for <a href="/wiki/Trial_by_ordeal" title="Trial by ordeal">trials by ordeal</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKristóMakk1996123_120-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKristóMakk1996123-120">&#91;120&#93;</a></sup> However, it cannot be determined whether Ladislaus became subject to veneration soon after his death, or if his cult emerged after he was canonized by <a href="/wiki/B%C3%A9la_III_of_Hungary" title="Béla III of Hungary">Béla III of Hungary</a> on 27 June 1192.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKlaniczay2002174,_182_121-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKlaniczay2002174,_182-121">&#91;121&#93;</a></sup> Béla had lived in the <a href="/wiki/Byzantine_Empire" title="Byzantine Empire">Byzantine</a> court, where Ladislaus's daughter, Irene, was venerated as a saint.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKlaniczay2002182_122-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKlaniczay2002182-122">&#91;122&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to Thomas the Archdeacon, <a href="/wiki/Pope_Innocent_III" title="Pope Innocent III">Pope Innocent III</a> declared that Ladislaus "should be enrolled in the catalogue of saints", but his report is unreliable, because <a href="/wiki/Celestine_III" class="mw-redirect" title="Celestine III">Celestine III</a> was pope at the time.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKlaniczay2002185_123-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKlaniczay2002185-123">&#91;123&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-124" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-124">&#91;124&#93;</a></sup> Celestine III's bulls and charters make no reference to Ladislaus's canonization, implying that Ladislaus was canonized without the Holy See's authorization.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKlaniczay2002185–186_125-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKlaniczay2002185–186-125">&#91;125&#93;</a></sup> The nearly contemporaneous <i><a href="/wiki/Regestrum_Varadinense" title="Regestrum Varadinense">Regestrum Varadinense</a></i> says that a <a href="/wiki/Serfdom" title="Serfdom">bondsman</a>, named "Tekus, son of the craftsman Dénes", opened Ladislaus's tomb at the beginning of the ceremony, after which Tekus was granted freedom.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKlaniczay2002186_98-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKlaniczay2002186-98">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup> Parts of Ladislaus's head and right hand were severed so that they could be distributed as relics.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKlaniczay2002186_98-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKlaniczay2002186-98">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup> The 15th-century silver <a href="/wiki/Reliquary" title="Reliquary">reliquary</a> that contains Ladislaus's head is displayed in the <a href="/wiki/Cathedral_Basilica_of_the_Assumption_of_Our_Lady,_Gy%C5%91r" class="mw-redirect" title="Cathedral Basilica of the Assumption of Our Lady, Győr">Győr Cathedral</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-126" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-126">&#91;126&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:765572.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/50/765572.jpg/220px-765572.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="109" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/50/765572.jpg/330px-765572.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/50/765572.jpg/440px-765572.jpg 2x" data-file-width="500" data-file-height="248" /></a><figcaption>Gold coin of King <a href="/wiki/Sigismund,_Holy_Roman_Emperor" title="Sigismund, Holy Roman Emperor">Sigismund of Hungary</a> depicting King Saint Ladislaus</figcaption></figure>
<p>Ladislaus's official legend, which was compiled after 1204,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKlaniczay2002186_98-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKlaniczay2002186-98">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup> attributes a number of <a href="/wiki/Miracles" class="mw-redirect" title="Miracles">miracles</a> to him.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELanziLanzi2004142_127-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELanziLanzi2004142-127">&#91;127&#93;</a></sup> According to one of his legends, a pestilence spread throughout the kingdom during Ladislaus's reign. Ladislaus prayed for a cure; he then shot an arrow into the air at random, hitting a herb which cured the illness.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELanziLanzi2004142_127-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELanziLanzi2004142-127">&#91;127&#93;</a></sup> This plant became known as "<a href="/wiki/Gentiana_cruciata" title="Gentiana cruciata">Saint Ladislaus's herb</a>" in Hungary.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELanziLanzi2004142_127-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELanziLanzi2004142-127">&#91;127&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Ladislaus is a <a href="/wiki/Patron_saint" title="Patron saint">patron saint</a> of Hungary, especially along the borders.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELanziLanzi2004142_127-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELanziLanzi2004142-127">&#91;127&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELászló1996145_128-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELászló1996145-128">&#91;128&#93;</a></sup> In particular, soldiers and the <a href="/wiki/Sz%C3%A9kelys" title="Székelys">Székely people</a> venerate him.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKristóMakk1996123_120-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKristóMakk1996123-120">&#91;120&#93;</a></sup> A late medieval legend says that Ladislaus appeared at the head of a Székely army fighting against and routing a plundering band of Tatars in 1345.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKristóMakk1996123_120-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKristóMakk1996123-120">&#91;120&#93;</a></sup> He is also called upon during times of pestilence.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELanziLanzi2004142_127-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELanziLanzi2004142-127">&#91;127&#93;</a></sup> He is often depicted as a mature, bearded man wearing a royal crown and holding a long sword or <a href="/wiki/Banner" title="Banner">banner</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELanziLanzi2004142_127-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELanziLanzi2004142-127">&#91;127&#93;</a></sup> He is also shown on his knees before a deer, or in the company of two angels.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELanziLanzi2004142_127-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELanziLanzi2004142-127">&#91;127&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>King <a href="/wiki/Sigismund,_Holy_Roman_Emperor" title="Sigismund, Holy Roman Emperor">Sigismund of Hungary</a> died on 1437, and as ordered in life, he was buried at Várad (now <a href="/wiki/Oradea" title="Oradea">Oradea</a>), next to the tomb of the King Saint Ladislaus, who was the ideal of the perfect monarch, warrior and Christian for that time and was deeply venerated by Sigismund.<sup id="cite_ref-129" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-129">&#91;129&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Herma_of_King_Saint_Ladislaus_and_Genetics">Herma of King Saint Ladislaus and Genetics</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ladislaus_I_of_Hungary&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Herma of King Saint Ladislaus and Genetics">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Szent_L%C3%A1szl%C3%B3_herm%C3%A1ja.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="King Saint Ladislaus of Hungary, knight, horse, spear, medieval, fresco, Transylvania" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/79/Szent_L%C3%A1szl%C3%B3_herm%C3%A1ja.jpg/220px-Szent_L%C3%A1szl%C3%B3_herm%C3%A1ja.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="326" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/79/Szent_L%C3%A1szl%C3%B3_herm%C3%A1ja.jpg/330px-Szent_L%C3%A1szl%C3%B3_herm%C3%A1ja.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/79/Szent_L%C3%A1szl%C3%B3_herm%C3%A1ja.jpg/440px-Szent_L%C3%A1szl%C3%B3_herm%C3%A1ja.jpg 2x" data-file-width="837" data-file-height="1240" /></a><figcaption>Herm of King Saint Ladislaus (15th century) containing his skull, held in the <a href="/wiki/Cathedral_Basilica_of_Gy%C5%91r" title="Cathedral Basilica of Győr">Cathedral Basilica of Győr</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>The skull <a href="/wiki/Relic" title="Relic">relic</a> in the Saint Ladislaus’ Herma preserved in the <a href="/wiki/Cathedral_Basilica_of_Gy%C5%91r" title="Cathedral Basilica of Győr">Cathedral Basilica of Győr</a>, is one of the most important relics for Hungarians.<sup id="cite_ref-Saint_Ladislaus_DNA_130-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Saint_Ladislaus_DNA-130">&#91;130&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>King Saint Ladislaus of Hungary completed the work of King <a href="/wiki/Stephen_I_of_Hungary" title="Stephen I of Hungary">Saint Stephen of Hungary</a>, he consolidated the Hungarian state power and strengthened the <a href="/wiki/Christianity" title="Christianity">Christianity</a>. His charismatic personality, strategic leadership and military talents resulted the termination of internal power struggles and foreign military threats. He was seen as the embodiment of the knight-king ideal to be emulated all over Europe. He was canonized in 1192 at the request of King <a href="/wiki/B%C3%A9la_III_of_Hungary" title="Béla III of Hungary">Béla III of Hungary</a>, and his body was exhumed to prepare relics from his skull and other skeletal remains as was the custom in that medieval times.<sup id="cite_ref-Saint_Ladislaus_DNA_130-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Saint_Ladislaus_DNA-130">&#91;130&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to historian György Szabados, <a href="/wiki/Gyula_L%C3%A1szl%C3%B3" title="Gyula László">Gyula László</a> proved already in 1965 that the face of the Saint Ladislaus’ Herma depicts King Béla III. The skull reconstruction of the <a href="/wiki/Basilica_of_the_Assumption_of_the_Blessed_Virgin_Mary" title="Basilica of the Assumption of the Blessed Virgin Mary">Royal Basilica's tomb</a> find in Székesfehérvár also shows the face of the Herma, which is not surprising, because King Ladislaus had already been dead for 97 years in 1192, so only the then living king from the same family, Béla III was worthy to sit as a model for the creation of the face of the Herma.<sup id="cite_ref-131" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-131">&#91;131&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The wooden herm containing the skull was damaged in a fire in 1406, but miraculously the skull has been preserved unharmed. Later it was placed into the current Herma created during the reign of King <a href="/wiki/Sigismund,_Holy_Roman_Emperor" title="Sigismund, Holy Roman Emperor">Sigismund</a>. In the 16th century, the relic had to be rescued from <a href="/wiki/Oradea" title="Oradea">Várad</a> due to the ravage of Transylvania by the protestants. In the first decades of the 17th century, it reached its current location in the Cathedral of <a href="/wiki/Gy%C5%91r" title="Győr">Győr</a> after passing through <a href="/wiki/Prague" title="Prague">Prague</a>, Pozsony (now <a href="/wiki/Bratislava" title="Bratislava">Bratislava</a>) and <a href="/wiki/Veszpr%C3%A9m" title="Veszprém">Veszprém</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Saint_Ladislaus_DNA_130-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Saint_Ladislaus_DNA-130">&#91;130&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The turbulent history events of the Herma raised doubts concerning the authenticity of the relic by historians and archaeologists.<sup id="cite_ref-Saint_Ladislaus_DNA_130-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Saint_Ladislaus_DNA-130">&#91;130&#93;</a></sup> At that time, the only <a href="/wiki/%C3%81rp%C3%A1d_dynasty" title="Árpád dynasty">Árpád dynasty</a> king, the remains of King <a href="/wiki/B%C3%A9la_III_of_Hungary" title="Béla III of Hungary">Béla III</a> had known and identified, because little earlier, the Institute of Hungarian Research determinated the whole genome data of King Béla III which was published in 2020.<sup id="cite_ref-Béla_III_DNA_132-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Béla_III_DNA-132">&#91;132&#93;</a></sup> Thus the Hungarian scholars were able to compare the Y chromosome sequence of the skull to that of King Béla III from whom a whole genome data was available.<sup id="cite_ref-Saint_Ladislaus_DNA_130-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Saint_Ladislaus_DNA-130">&#91;130&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 4 June 2021, Endre Neparáczki collected a sample from the skull of the herm, during his researches so far, they isolated from the sample one of the best own extracted endogenous DNA.<sup id="cite_ref-:6_133-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:6-133">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup> The Institute of Hungarian Research defined the genetic composition of the skull found in the herm and it was published in 2023. The result supported the originality of Saint Ladislaus relic, the <a href="/wiki/Human_Y-chromosome_DNA_haplogroup" title="Human Y-chromosome DNA haplogroup">Y chromosome</a> of the skull belongs to the exclusive haplogroup of the Árpád-dynasty R-ARP (R1a1a1b2a2a1c3a3b) and the kinship analysis detected the skull is at five generations distance from King Béla III.<sup id="cite_ref-Saint_Ladislaus_DNA_130-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Saint_Ladislaus_DNA-130">&#91;130&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-:6_133-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:6-133">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>The R-ARP sub-haplogroup belongs to the R-Z2123 clade, the <a href="/wiki/Phylogenetics" title="Phylogenetics">phylogenetic</a> analysis suggested a <a href="/wiki/Bronze_Age" title="Bronze Age">Bronze Age</a> <a href="/wiki/Bactria%E2%80%93Margiana_Archaeological_Complex" title="Bactria–Margiana Archaeological Complex">BMAC</a> origin of the R-Z2123 sub-haplogroup which belongs to the R-Z2125 clade, which was detected in individuals from the Middle-Late <a href="/wiki/Bronze_Age" title="Bronze Age">Bronze Age</a> on the Caspian Steppe, connected to the <a href="/wiki/Potapovka_culture" title="Potapovka culture">Potapovka</a>, <a href="/wiki/Sintashta_culture" title="Sintashta culture">Sintashta</a> and <a href="/wiki/Andronovo_culture" title="Andronovo culture">Andronovo</a> cultures. In the <a href="/wiki/Iron_Age" title="Iron Age">Iron Age</a>, this haplogroup was detected in the Turan basin and in <a href="/wiki/Scytho-Siberian_world" title="Scytho-Siberian world">Scytho-Siberians</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Minusinsk_Hollow" title="Minusinsk Hollow">Minusinsk Basin</a>, later among the <a href="/wiki/Xiongnu" title="Xiongnu">Asian Huns</a> (Xiongnus) and up until the <a href="/wiki/Middle_Ages" title="Middle Ages">Middle Ages</a> in <a href="/wiki/Mongolia" title="Mongolia">Mongolia</a> which indicates an eastward and southward spread of the haplogroup. The first appearance of R-Z2125 in the <a href="/wiki/Pannonian_Basin" title="Pannonian Basin">Carpathian Basin</a> was detected in 5th-century <a href="/wiki/Huns" title="Huns">European Huns</a>, and 7th–8th-century <a href="/wiki/Pannonian_Avars" title="Pannonian Avars">Avars</a>, but it also arrived with the <a href="/wiki/Hungarian_conquest_of_the_Carpathian_Basin" title="Hungarian conquest of the Carpathian Basin">conquering Hungarians</a>, in the 9th–10th century including <a href="/wiki/%C3%81rp%C3%A1d" title="Árpád">Árpád</a> and his family.<sup id="cite_ref-Saint_Ladislaus_DNA_130-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Saint_Ladislaus_DNA-130">&#91;130&#93;</a></sup> The basic premise of the Hungarian medieval chronicle tradition (for example in the <a href="/wiki/Chronicon_Pictum" title="Chronicon Pictum">Chronicon Pictum</a>) that the Huns, i.e. the <a href="/wiki/Hungarians" title="Hungarians">Hungarians</a> coming out twice from <a href="/wiki/Scythians" title="Scythians">Scythia</a>, the guiding principle was the Hun-Hungarian continuity.<sup id="cite_ref-:3_134-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:3-134">&#91;134&#93;</a></sup> The genomic analyses of the <a href="/wiki/List_of_Hungarian_monarchs" title="List of Hungarian monarchs">Hungarian royal</a> <a href="/wiki/%C3%81rp%C3%A1d_dynasty" title="Árpád dynasty">Árpád family</a> members are in line with the reported conquering Hungarian-Hun origin of the dynasty in harmony with their Y-chromosomal phylogenetic connections.<sup id="cite_ref-Saint_Ladislaus_DNA_130-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Saint_Ladislaus_DNA-130">&#91;130&#93;</a></sup>
</p>

Cambridge University Press. p.&#160;59.</cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=bookitem&amp;rft.atitle=Ladislaus%2C+Saint&amp;rft.btitle=Encyclop%C3%A6dia+Britannica&amp;rft.pages=59&amp;rft.edition=11th&amp;rft.pub=Cambridge+University+Press&amp;rft.date=1911&amp;rft.aulast=Bain&amp;rft.aufirst=Robert+Nisbet&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3ALadislaus+I+of+Hungary" class="Z3988"></span></li>

<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1054258005"><div class="refbegin" style="">
<ul><li>St. Ladislaus (in <i>The Catholic Encyclopedia</i>) <a rel="nofollow" class="external autonumber" href="http://www.newadvent.org/cathen/08737a.htm">[1]</a></li>
<li><i>St Ladislaus, King of Hungary</i> (a painting from around 1326 in the Museo della Consolazione in <a href="/wiki/Altomonte" title="Altomonte">Altomonte</a>, <a href="/wiki/Italy" title="Italy">Italy</a>) <a rel="nofollow" class="external autonumber" href="http://www.wga.hu/html_m/s/simone/4altars/5agostin/7altomon.html">[2]</a></li>
<li><i>Üdvözlégy, kegyelmes Szent László király</i> (Hail, Merciful King St Ladislaus) (a hymn to King St Ladislaus) <a rel="nofollow" class="external autonumber" href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=PYirs9Qmi_A">[3]</a></li></ul>
